, except that now the conformal dimensions can take negative values, which renders the theory non-unitary.

Degenerate representations of the superconformal algebra for
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
occur at momenta

\hat{p}_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{i\left(r\hat{b}^{-1}-s\hat{b}\right)}{2},~~~~r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1},

(94)

where, again, we have a null NS state when
r-s\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, and a null R state when
r-s\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
. Notice that, in contrast to the spacelike case, the degenerate representations ‘overlap’ with the support of the timelike Liouville spectrum (modulo the
\epsilon
shift, as in the non-supersymmetric timelike case
Ribault:2015sxa
). In particular the field with
\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
has
\hat{h}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}=0
and it is tempting to identify it with the usual identity operator. However, this is subtle and we are going to return to this issue when we discuss the structure constants.

We depict the various spectra in figures
5
and
6
.

\frac{\hat{Q}}{2}
\hat{p}\in i\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}-\epsilon
\hat{p}_{\langle r,s\rangle},r-s\in 2\mathbb{Z}
\hat{p}
NS-sector

Figure 5
:
The expected physical NS-spectrum in timelike
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville theory (blue) and the degenerate representations for the NS algebra (magenta) for central charge values
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
(or
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
). The corresponding expressions are given in (
92
), (
94
).

\frac{\hat{Q}}{2}
\hat{p}\in i\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}-\epsilon
\hat{p}_{\langle r,s\rangle},r-s\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1
\hat{p}
R-sector

Figure 6
:
The expected physical R-spectrum in timelike
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville theory (blue) and the degenerate representations of the R algebra (magenta) for central charge values
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
(or
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
). The corresponding expressions are given in (
92
), (
94
).

NS-sector.

In the NS-sector of the theory, we denote the primaries with conformal dimension
\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{NS}}
given in (
92
) as
\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{NS}}

21
21

21

In terms of the Lagrangian (
90
) we may want to think of them as the normal ordered exponentials

\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{NS}}=:\mathrm{e}^{(-\frac{\widehat{Q}}{{2}}-i\hat{p})\phi}:.
.
We distinguish again the three operators generated by the super Poincaré subalgebra

\widehat{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}=\widehat{G}_{-1/2}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}~,\quad\widehat{\widetilde{\Lambda}}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}=\widehat{\widetilde{G}}_{-1/2}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}~,\quad\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}=\widehat{G}_{-1/2}\widehat{\widetilde{G}}_{-1/2}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}~.

(95)

Their OPE with the super Virasoro stress tensor and the supercurrent are analogous to the spacelike case (
20
) with the obvious replacements.

R-sector.

Similarly in the R-sector, we introduce again the combinations

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0}=\widehat{G}_{0}+i\widehat{\widetilde{G}}_{0}~,\quad\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{0}=\widehat{G}_{0}-i\widehat{\widetilde{G}}_{0}~,

(96)

which satisfy the fermionic harmonic oscillator commutation relations (
25
). On the doubly degenerate operator
\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}
they act as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},+}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{i\pi}{4}}\sqrt{4\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R}}-\frac{\hat{c}}{4}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},-}~,\quad\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},-}=0~,

(97a)

\displaystyle\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{0}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},-}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}\sqrt{4\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R}}-\frac{\hat{c}}{4}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},+}~,\quad\ \ \widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{0}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R},+}=0~,

(97b)

with
\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}^{\mathrm{R}}
given in (
92
).

3.2
Three-point functions

In this section we describe our main new results, which are the explicit expressions for the
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
structure constants of
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory. We view these results as part of the fully quantum definition of the timelike theory, together with the spectra described in the previous section. Just as in ordinary timelike Liouville theory
Zamolodchikov:2005fy
, the key idea relies on the observation that, even though the spacelike structure constants (
61
), (
82
)
cannot
be analytically continued as a function of the central charge, the basic shift relations that determine those data (
2.2.1
), (
70
) can. The strategy then is to look for new solutions to those analytically continued shift relations at
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
. We will systematize this analytic continuation by implementing the so-called ‘Virasoro-Wick Rotation’ symmetry, first discussed at the level of the shift relations for the Virasoro modular and fusion kernels in
Ribault:2023vqs
. This procedure will then clearly reveal how to construct new solutions for the structure constants valid at
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
from the solutions valid at
c\geqslant 9
.

3.2.1
Virasoro–Wick Rotation

Following
Ribault:2023vqs
, we define the Virasoro–Wick Rotation (VWR) as the (anti-)involution map

\left\{\begin{aligned} p&\rightarrow\hat{p}=ip\\
b&\rightarrow\hat{b}=-ib\end{aligned}\right\}

(98)

on the parameters
(p,b)
characterizing the (holomorphic) conformal dimension
h
and the central charge
c
respectively. In usual bosonic Liouville theory, the central charge and conformal dimensions are parametrized as in (
179
), which means that under VWR they map to

\mathcal{N}=0:~~~~\left\{\begin{aligned} c&\rightarrow\hat{c}=26-c\\
h_{p}&\rightarrow\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}=1-h_{p}\end{aligned}\right\}\ .

(99)

Similarly, the
\mathcal{N}=1
theory we parametrize as in (
3
), (
11
) and therefore

\mathcal{N}=1:~~~~\left\{\begin{aligned} c&\rightarrow\hat{c}=10-c\\
h^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p}&\rightarrow\hat{h}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}=\frac{1}{2}-h^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p}\\
h^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}&\rightarrow\hat{h}^{\mathrm{R}}_{\hat{p}}=\frac{5}{8}-h^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}\end{aligned}\right\}\ .

(100)

All CFT quantities of interest (structure constants, correlation functions etc.) depend in principle on
(h,c)
, which are
even
functions w.r.t the parametrization
(p,b)
. Therefore, applying the VWR map
twice
leaves all the corresponding expressions unaffected. It is interesting however to study the transformation of the CFT quantities under a
single
VWR, assuming some analyticity in
p,b
. The structure constants of Liouville theory – such as the DOZZ formula or the
\mathcal{N}=1
structure constants (
61
), (
82
) – are famously non-analytic in
b
, and hence a single VWR is ill defined
22
22

22

Here we implicitly assume that we start with either a purely real or purely imaginary value of
b
, before performing the VWR.
. On the other hand, the basic shift relations that determine those data, such as eqns (
2.2.1
), (
70
) in the
\mathcal{N}=1
case, are analytic in
b
and possess interesting properties under a single VWR which we now explain.

For the NS-sector, let us return back to the main shift relations (
2.2.1
) or (
2.2.1
) that determine the structure constants. The ‘bootstrap functions’
(\kappa_{\mathrm{NS}},\lambda_{\mathrm{NS}})
and
(\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathrm{NS}},\widetilde{\lambda}_{\mathrm{NS}})
defined in (
287
), (
288
) and (
2.2.1
) can be shown to obey the following non-trivial relations

\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})=\frac{\left(\frac{p_{1}+b}{p_{1}-b}\right)^{2}}{\kappa_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-ib)}(-ip_{1}|-ip_{2},-ip_{3})}\ ,\ \widetilde{\lambda}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})=\frac{\left(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}-b}\right)^{2}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-ib)}(-ip_{1}|-ip_{2},-ip_{3})}~.

(101)

Similarly for the R-sector, the bootstrap function

\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}
that determines the main shift relations (
70
) and defined in (
72
), satisfies

\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1}|p_{2};p_{3})=\frac{1}{\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(-ib)}(-ip_{1}|ip_{2};-ip_{3})}~.

(102)

These equations demonstrate an almost inverse relation between the bootstrap functions evaluated at the original arguments and the bootstrap functions evaluated at the VWR-ed arguments (appropriately ‘coupled’, in the NS-sector case). It is exactly this feature, as we will see, that will allow us to construct the timelike solutions for the structure constants as, roughly speaking, the inverse of the spacelike ones. This is reminiscent of the almost inverse relation between the timelike and spacelike structure constants in ordinary bosonic Liouville theory
Kostov:2005kk
, which can be explained exactly along the same lines
23
23

23

For completeness, we present this derivation and the analogous equations for the bootstrap function in
\mathcal{N}=0
Liouville in Appendix
B
(c.f. eqns (
199
),(
202
)).
. We will describe concretely the construction in the
\mathcal{N}=1
case in the next sections.

More generally, in
Ribault:2023vqs
it was shown that a particular class of shift relations that determine the Virasoro modular and fusion kernels behave in a similar fashion under VWR. Therefore, one can in principle leverage this feature to construct analogous ‘timelike’ solutions for the crossing kernels with (Virasoro) central charge
c\leqslant 1
, starting from the kernels valid at
c\geqslant 25
. It can actually be shown that the structure constants of bosonic Liouville theory arise as special cases of the more general fusion and modular kernels
Teschner:2001rv
;
Collier:2019weq
, which explains the applicability of the VWR from this broader framework to the specific quantities of Liouville theory. In the
\mathcal{N}=1
case, it is expected that a similar connection between the fusion and modular kernels and Liouville’s structure constant is also true, although it is not as concretely understood to date as in the non-supersymmetric case
24
24

24

In Appendix
E
we discuss the explicit relation of the NS fusion kernel with the spacelike NS structure constants of Liouville theory.
.

3.2.2
NS-sector

We start by discussing the operator product in the NS-sector, which we normalize in the same way as in the spacelike theory

\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(z)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(0)\sim\int_{i\mathbb{R}_{+}-\epsilon}\frac{\text{d}\hat{p}}{i}\frac{(z\bar{z})^{\hat{h}_{\hat{p}}-\hat{h}_{\hat{p}_{1}}-\hat{h}_{\hat{p}_{2}}}}{\widehat{B}_{\text{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p})}\Big(\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p})\left[\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}(0)\right]_{\mathrm{ee}}\cr-\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p})\left[\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}(0)\right]_{\mathrm{oo}}\Big).

(103)

The corresponding two- and three-point functions that define
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory in the NS-sector take the form

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1})[\delta(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})+\delta(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})]~,

(104a)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(z_{1})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(z_{2})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}{|z_{12}|^{2\hat{h}_{1+2-3}}|z_{23}|^{2\hat{h}_{2+3-1}}|z_{31}|^{2\hat{h}_{3+1-2}}}~,

(104b)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(z_{1})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(z_{2})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}{|z_{12}|^{2\hat{h}_{1+2-3}+1}|z_{23}|^{2\hat{h}_{2+3-1}-1}|z_{31}|^{2\hat{h}_{3+1-2}+1}}~.

(104c)

Our goal is to derive explcit expressions for these data starting from basic constraints coming from representation theory, exactly parallel to the spacelike case. More specifically, our strategy is to look for
new
solutions to the main shift relations (
2.2.1
) in the timelike regime
b=-i\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
. To achieve this, we will implement the properties of the shift relations under Virasoro Wick Rotation, as discussed in section
3.2.1
.

For
b\in(0,1]
or
c\geqslant 9
, we have already found solutions to the main shift relations (i.e. the spacelike structure constants (
51
),(
61
)). In particular, let us express them as solutions to the alternative set of independent NS shift relations (
2.2.1
). We then have

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{C}^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}+b,p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}+b)}{\tilde{C}^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-b,p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-b)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{C^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1})}{\widetilde{C}^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-b,p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(b)}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-b)}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\lambda}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})~.

(105)

The functions
\widetilde{\kappa}_{\text{NS}}^{(b)},\widetilde{\lambda}_{\text{NS}}^{(b)}
are given in (
2.2.1
) and are analytic in both
b
and the
p_{i}
’s. They further obey the ‘inverse’ property (
101
) under VWR which we will shortly harness.

For
b=-i\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
, or
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
, the CFT data (
104
) satisfy the
same
shift relations in the corresponding central charge regime. Let us express those as
unknown
solutions to, instead, the original set of NS shift relations (
2.2.1
), namely

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{{C}}_{\text{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b}),\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b}))}{\widehat{{C}}_{\text{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}),\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}={\kappa}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})~,

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\text{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1})}{\widehat{{C}}_{\text{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}),\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}={\lambda}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})~.

(106)

There is an additional set of shift relations involving shifts by
b^{-1}=i\hat{b}^{-1}
, and hence identical to (
3.2.2
) with the substitution
\hat{b}\rightarrow-\hat{b}^{-1}
. The two sets of shift relations in
\hat{b},\hat{b}^{-1}
are incommensurable for
\hat{b}^{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}
, which is what we assume henceforth.

We will now derive the new timelike solutions by massaging equation (
3.2.2
) into (
3.2.2
). We start by relabeling
b=\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}
in (
3.2.2
) and using the ‘inverse’ property (
101
). This yields

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}+\hat{b},p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}+\hat{b})}{\tilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-\hat{b},p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-\hat{b})}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})=\frac{\left(\frac{p_{1}+\hat{b}}{p_{1}-\hat{b}}\right)^{2}}{\kappa_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(-ip_{1}|-ip_{2},-ip_{3})}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{C^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1})}{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-\hat{b},p_{2},p_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(p_{1}-\hat{b})}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\lambda}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(p_{1}|p_{2},p_{3})=\frac{\left(\frac{p_{1}}{p_{1}-\hat{b}}\right)^{2}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(-ip_{1}|-ip_{2},-ip_{3})}~.

(107)

If we now rename
p_{k}\rightarrow i\hat{p}_{k}
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b})),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b}))}{\tilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}=\frac{\left(\frac{\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b})}{\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b})}\right)^{2}}{\kappa_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{C^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1})}{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}=\frac{\left(\frac{\hat{p}_{1}}{\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b})}\right)^{2}}{\lambda_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}~,

(108)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b})}{\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b})}\right)^{2}\frac{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b})),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}{\tilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b}),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+(-i\hat{b}))}={\kappa_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}\ ,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\hat{p}_{1}}{\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b})}\right)^{2}\frac{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}),i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-(-i\hat{b}))}{C^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1})}={\lambda_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})}~.

(109)

This system of shift relations, together with its corresponding one with
\hat{b}

\rightarrow-\hat{b}^{-1}
,
has exactly the form (
3.2.2
) that we initially set out to solve. A consistent choice for the new solutions then reads

\displaystyle\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{p}^{2}B^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p})}~,

(110a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\rm NS}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{i}{\widetilde{C}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})}~,

(110b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\widetilde{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\rm NS}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{i}{C^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})}~,

(110c)

or, explicitly

\displaystyle\widehat{B}^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(\hat{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sinh(\pi\hat{b}\hat{p})\sinh(\pi\hat{b}^{-1}\hat{p})}{\hat{p}^{2}}\ ,

(111a)

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\rm NS}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b})^{3}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(2(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b}))}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}\left(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}\right)}{\Gamma^{\rm R}_{\hat{b}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{1}\pm i\hat{p}_{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}~,

(111b)

\displaystyle\widehat{\widetilde{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\rm NS}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{2i\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b})^{3}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(2(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b}))}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}\left(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}\right)}{\Gamma^{\rm NS}_{\hat{b}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}-\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{1}\pm i\hat{p}_{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}~.

(111c)

These are our main results for the timelike NS structure constants. It is evident from the above that, as in the non-supersymmetric setting, the
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike structure constants are
not
the analytic continuation of their spacelike counterparts. It is also interesting that the structure constant
\widehat{C}_{\rm NS}
, corresponding roughly to the integer-descendant module (c.f. the OPE (
103
)), is given by the inverse of
\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}
in the spacelike case (which corresponds to the half-integer descendant module), and similarly for the pair
\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\rm NS}\leftrightarrow C_{\mathrm{NS}}
. This fact traces back to the way the shift relations are coupled under VWR, as dictated by (
101
).

Notice that we have kept explicit a
\pm
ambiguity in the definition of the structure constants, as the shift relations are insensitive to this choice
25
25

25

Of course, similar ambiguities come with the two-point normalization, though we will not discuss any particular choice at this stage. We will return to this issue in section
3.3
where we discuss the connection with the Minimal Models.
. We will return to this point in section
4
. On the other hand, the choice for the overall
b-
dependent factor (which the shift relations are also insensitive to) is essentially fixed by the spacelike expressions in our conventions
26
26

26

We simply chose an extra factor of
i
in (
110b
) in order to cancel the
i
in the definition of
\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}
, while in (
110c
) we choose an
i
to make the overall constant look similar to the spacelike case.
.

Properties of the three-point functions.

The expressions (
111
) are the
unique
solutions to the NS shift relations in the superconformal central charge regime
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
, exactly in the same sense that the bosonic timelike structure constants are
Zamolodchikov:1995aa
;
Teschner:1995yf
. In particular, modulo a momentum-independent factor, they are the unique solutions with the following features:

•

Continuous in
\hat{b}
, and invariant under
\hat{b}\leftrightarrow-\hat{b}^{-1}
,

•

Meromorphic in the momenta
\hat{p}_{i}
,

•

Permutation symmetric under the exchange of any two momenta,

•

Reflection symmetric under any
\hat{p}_{i}\rightarrow-\hat{p}_{i}
.

It is now interesting to ask about the limits
\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
in the expressions (
111
). It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\lim_{\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}\widehat{C}_{\rm NS}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle\neq\delta(\hat{p}_{1}\pm\hat{p}_{2})~,

\displaystyle\lim_{\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}}\widehat{\widetilde{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\rm NS}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle\neq 0.

(112)

In particular, both of these limits turn out to be finite (and non-zero). In other words, we observe that not only the ‘diagonal’ structure of the two-point functions is not recovered from the limit
\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
of
\widehat{C}_{\rm NS}
, but also the fact that the identity module is built purely out of integer-level descendants is not encoded in the expression for
\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\rm NS}
. This is in contrast to the analogous statements we discussed in the spacelike case (c.f. (
62
)) and reflects the fact that the operator with momentum
\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
is
not
to be identified with the usual identity operator. These apparent ‘problematic’ features of the theory are attributed to its non-unitary nature, analogously to the non-supersymmetric setup
Ribault:2015sxa
;
Harlow:2011ny
.

Instead, the correct statement is that the two-point functions
\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle
,
\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle
are
not
obtained by analytic continuation from the corresponding three-point functions, and we define the theory by imposing

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1})}{\hat{p}_{1}^{2}}\left(\delta(\hat{p_{1}}-\hat{p}_{2})+\delta(\hat{p_{1}}+\hat{p}_{2})\right),~~\text{and}~~

(113)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

(114)

3.2.3
R-sector

In the R-sector we proceed similarly. We define the corresponding two- and three-point functions for
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
as

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\widehat{B}_{\text{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1})[\delta(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})+\epsilon\,\delta(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})]~,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(z_{1})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(z_{2})\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})}{|z_{12}|^{2\hat{h}_{[1]+[2]-3}}|z_{23}|^{2\hat{h}_{[2]+3-[1]}}|z_{31}|^{2\hat{h}_{3+[1]-[2]}}}~,

(115)

and we define again the following combinations of three-point correlators

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\langle\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{R,+}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{R,+}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle+\langle\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{R,-}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{R,-}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\right)~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\langle\widehat{V}_{p_{1}}^{\mathrm{R,+}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{R,+}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle-\langle\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{1}}^{\mathrm{R,-}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{2}}^{\mathrm{R,-}}\widehat{V}_{\hat{p}_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\right)~.

(116)

We will determine those data starting from the basic Ramond sector shift relations (
70
), (
73
) which are analytic in
b
, and use their properties under VWR.

For
b\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
or
c\geqslant 9
, the spacelike solutions (
76
), (
82
) satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(p_{1}+\frac{b}{2},p_{2};p_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1}+\frac{b}{2})}{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(p_{1}-\frac{b}{2},p_{2};p_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1}-\frac{b}{2})}

\displaystyle=\kappa^{(b)}_{\mathrm{R}}\left(p_{1}|p_{2};p_{3}\right)~,

(117)

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(p_{1}+\frac{b}{2},p_{2};p_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1}+\frac{b}{2})}{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(p_{1}-\frac{b}{2},p_{2};p_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1}-\frac{b}{2})}

\displaystyle=\kappa^{(b)}_{\mathrm{R}}\left(p_{1}|-p_{2};p_{3}\right).

(118)

The function
\kappa^{(b)}_{\mathrm{R}}
is given in (
72
) and is manifestly analytic in both
p_{i}
’s and
b
. In particular, as we discussed, it satisfies the ‘inverse’ property (
102
) which we will put to work shortly.

For
b=-i\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
, or
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
, the Ramond data (
115
), (
116
) satisfy the
same
shift relations in the corresponding central charge regime, namely

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2})}{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2})}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})~,

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2})}{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/\widehat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2})}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|-\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})~,

(119)

together with the complement shift relations with
\hat{b}\rightarrow-\hat{b}^{-1}
.

In the same way as before, one can massage equation (
118
) into (
119
) by renaming
b\rightarrow\hat{b}
,
p_{k}\rightarrow i\hat{p}_{k}
, together with the ‘inverse’ property (
102
). This yields

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}),i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}))}{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2},i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}))}

\displaystyle={\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|-\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})}~,

\displaystyle\frac{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}),i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}-\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}))}{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}),i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})^{2}/B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(i(\hat{p}_{1}+\frac{(-i\hat{b})}{2}))}

\displaystyle={\kappa_{\mathrm{R}}^{(-i\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1}|\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})}~.

(120)

Comparing this with (
119
), a consistent choice for the new solutions reads

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p})}~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})}~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})}~.

(121)

or, explicitly

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{\text{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p})

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2}\cosh(\pi\hat{b}\hat{p})\cosh(\pi\hat{b}^{-1}\hat{p})~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{R},\epsilon}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})+\epsilon\,\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pm\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b})^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j})}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(2(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b}))\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pm\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b})^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3})\prod_{j=1}^{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j})}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\rm NS}(2(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b}))\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}~.

(122)

These are our main results for the
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville structure constants in the Ramond sector. In particular, it is evident again that they are
not
given by the analytic continuation of their spacelike counterparts. We have also kept explicit a
\pm
ambiguity and, just as in the NS-sector, the choice for the overall
b-
dependent factor (which the shift relations are insensitive to) is essentially fixed by the spacelike expressions in our conventions.

Properties of the three-point functions.

The expressions (
122
) are again the
unique
solutions to the shift relations (
119
) valid for
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
(modulo a momentum-independent factor) with the following features:

•

Continuous in
\hat{b}
, and invariant under
\hat{b}\leftrightarrow-\hat{b}^{-1}

•

Meromorphic in the momenta
\hat{p}_{i}

•

Permutation symmetric under the exchange of
\hat{p}_{1}\leftrightarrow\hat{p}_{2}

•

Reflection symmetric under the NS-sector momentum
\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow-\hat{p}_{3}
, while

\frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},-\hat{p_{2}};\hat{p}_{3})}{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})}=1\ ,\ \ \frac{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(-\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p_{2}};\hat{p}_{3})}{\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})}=1~.

(123)

It is straightforward to check finally that also in this case the limit
\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}
of the structure constants does
not
reproduce the diagonal structure of the two-point function, and to define the theory we simply impose

\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle={\rho^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{R}}(i\hat{p}_{1})}\left(\delta(\hat{p_{1}}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm\delta(\hat{p_{1}}+\hat{p}_{2})\right)~.

(124)

3.3
Generalized
\mathcal{N}=1
Minimal Models

We now turn to the connection of
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory with the
\mathcal{N}=1
superconformal minimal models. In the non-supersymmetric case
Zamolodchikov:2005fy
, a connection of the timelike theory was established with the so-called
generalized minimal models
which have roughly the features of the usual minimal models except certain analyticity in the central charge and the spectrum is assumed. We will see that the same connection is true in the
\mathcal{N}=1
case. We start with a small review of the ordinary
\mathcal{N}=1
minimal models
Friedan:1984rv
.

The
\mathcal{N}=1
superconformal minimal models are described by a pair of positive integers
(p,p^{\prime}>p)
with
p,p^{\prime}\geqslant 2
. The unitary series is characterized by
p^{\prime}=p+2
, whereas more generally one can have either
(p,p^{\prime})
odd and coprime, or
(p,p^{\prime})
even, with
(p/2,p^{\prime}/2)
coprime and
(p-p^{\prime})/2
odd
DiFrancesco:1988xz

27
27

27

This last requirement comes from modular invariance; see also Appendix B of
Klebanov:2003wg
on this point.
. The central charge is characterized by rational
\hat{b}^{2}
, i.e.

\hat{b}^{2}=p^{\prime}/p\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\hat{c}_{p,p^{\prime}}=1-\frac{2(p-p^{\prime})^{2}}{pp^{\prime}}.

(125)

The primary operators
\mathcal{O}_{r,s}
are labelled by two positive integers
r,s
subject to

1\leqslant s\leqslant p^{\prime}-1,\quad 1\leqslant r\leqslant p-1,\quad rp^{\prime}\geqslant sp,

(126)

and are identified as
\mathcal{O}_{r,s}\equiv\mathcal{O}_{p-r,p^{\prime}-s}
. Their conformal dimensions read

h_{(r,s)}=\frac{\left(rp^{\prime}-sp\right)^{2}-\left(p-p^{\prime}\right)^{2}}{8pp^{\prime}}+\frac{1-(-1)^{r-s}}{32},

(127)

and correspond to NS operators for even
s-r
, and to R operators for odd
s-r
. A prototypical example that belongs in the unitary class is the tri-critical Ising model with
p=3,p^{\prime}=5
. It contains two operators in the NS-sector, namely
\mathcal{O}_{1,1}
(i.e. the vacuum) and
\mathcal{O}_{1,3}
, and two operators in the R-sector,
\mathcal{O}_{1,2}
,
\mathcal{O}_{2,1}
28
28

28

Of course it is known that the tri-critical Ising model also happens to be minimal w.r.t the Virasoro subalgebra.

Qiu:1986if
.

Due the existence of null states, the operators of the
(p,p^{\prime})
minimal model are characterized by a truncated fusion algebra in the OPE of two fields, analogously to the usual Virasoro minimal models
Belavin:1984vu
;
Friedan:1983xq
. In particular, it has been shown that the Coulomb gas formalism of the Virasoro minimal models, as prescribed by Dotsenko and Fateev
Dotsenko:1984nm
;
Dotsenko:1984ad
;
Dotsenko:1985hi
, can be extended to the case of
\mathcal{N}=1
superconformal minimal models, allowing in principle for the extraction of explicit expressions for the OPE coefficients
Kitazawa:1987za
;
Alvarez-Gaume:1991nvs
;
Mussardo:1987eq
;
Jayaraman:1989as
.

Similar to what was observed in
Zamolodchikov:2005fy
, we will see that the timelike structure constants of
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville theory, when certain momenta are evaluated at the values of the degenerate representations, agree with the OPE coefficients of the
\mathcal{N}=1
minimal models. However, just as in
Zamolodchikov:2005fy
, one
cannot
recover the truncated fusion rules from the
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike expressions for the structure constants. It is in this sense that the latter should be understood as describing a
formal
series of the so-called
generalized

\mathcal{N}=1
minimal models, where the central charge is treated as a continuous parameter (in particular,
\hat{b}^{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}
) and the (formal) spectrum of primary operators
\mathcal{O}_{r,s}
is not characterized by a truncated fusion algebra
Belavin:2008vc
.

To establish this connection we will have to switch to the canonical normalization of operators in timelike Liouville theory, namely one where all the two-point functions are unit-normalized. This is different than the one we discussed in section
3
. Doing that consistently, we obtain the following expressions for the structure constants for
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
(or
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
):

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{NS}}(\hat{p})=1\ ,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})=A_{\mathrm{NS}}\frac{\left[\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\right]^{1/2}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{1}\pm i\hat{p}_{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{NS}}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})=2iA_{\mathrm{NS}}\frac{\left[\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\right]^{1/2}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{1}\pm i\hat{p}_{2}\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}

(128)

for the NS-sector, and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{R}}(\hat{p})=1\ ,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{even}}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})=A_{\mathrm{R}}\times

\displaystyle~~\frac{\left[\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\right]^{1/2}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{odd}}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})=A_{\mathrm{R}}\times

\displaystyle~~\frac{\left[\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{3}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2}\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\pm 2i\hat{p}_{j}+\hat{b}^{-1}\right)\right]^{1/2}}{\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)\Gamma_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{-1}}{2}\pm i(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm i\hat{p}_{3}\right)}

(129)

for the R-sector. It is straightforward to verify that these expressions satisfy the main shift relations (
2.2.1
), (
70
) respectively for
b=-i\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}\in\mathbb{R}_{(0,1]}
.

Following
Zamolodchikov:2005fy
, we will determine the overall constants
A_{\mathrm{NS}},A_{\mathrm{R}}
from the requirement that the two-point function of identical operators yields unity. Denoting
\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\equiv\frac{i(\hat{b}^{-1}-\hat{b})}{2}
, somewhat surprisingly we find that the two constants are given by the same expression

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\hat{p},\hat{p},\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\right)=1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \quad\quad A_{\mathrm{NS}}\equiv\frac{\hat{b}^{\frac{\hat{b}^{-2}-\hat{b}^{2}}{2}-1}\left[\gamma\left(\frac{\hat{b}^{2}+1}{2}\right)\gamma\left(\frac{\hat{b}^{-2}-1}{2}\right)\right]^{1/2}}{\Upsilon_{\hat{b}}^{\mathrm{R}}(\hat{b})}\ ,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{even}}\left(\hat{p},\hat{p};\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\right)=\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{odd}}\left(\hat{p},-\hat{p};\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\right)=1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ A_{\mathrm{R}}=A_{\mathrm{NS}}~.

(130)

Note that we do not claim that we somehow recovered the
diagonal
structure of the two-point function in this normalization. This is not possible in general, regardless of the chosen normalization of the timelike structure constants as we emphasized in section
3
. Having said that, it is understood that the quantities
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\hat{p},\hat{p},\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\right),\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\mathrm{even}}\left(\hat{p},\hat{p};\hat{p}_{\mathds{1}}\right)
in (
3.3
) are well-defined and finite.

It might initially appear problematic that the structure constants in the normalization (
128
), (
129
) involve square roots, as this comes at the cost of meromorphicity. However, the advantage is that these expressions match with the corresponding OPE coefficients of the
\mathcal{N}=1
minimal models, when certain momenta are specified at degenerate values. Indeed, one can compare directly e.g. with the expressions in the NS-sector written down in
Kitazawa:1987za
;
Alvarez-Gaume:1991nvs
(or, slightly more clearly, with the refined expressions given in
Fredenhagen:2007tk
29
29

29

See equations (111), (113) in Appendix A of the paper.
) and obtain an explicit match with our formulas (
128
), (
3.3
).

A concrete example of this matching can be seen in the general form of the OPE between a degenerate NS field
\mathcal{O}_{1,3}
with a general NS field
\mathcal{O}_{r,s}
at a given minimal model with
\hat{b}^{2}=p^{\prime}/p
, which obeys the general fusion rule (assuming all
\mathcal{O}
’s are unit-normalized)
Friedan:1984rv

\mathcal{O}_{1,3}(z)\mathcal{O}_{r,s}(0)=\sum_{\varepsilon=\{1,0,-1\}}C_{(1,3)(r,s)}\text{}^{(r,s+2\varepsilon)}(z\bar{z})^{h_{(r,s+2\varepsilon)}-h_{(r,s)}-h_{(1,3)}}\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s+2\varepsilon}\right](0)

(131)

with (see e.g.
Belavin:2008vc
)

\displaystyle C_{(1,3)(r,s)}\text{}^{(r,s+2\varepsilon)}=\left[\frac{\gamma\left(\frac{1+\hat{b}^{2}}{2}\right)\gamma\left(\varepsilon i\hat{b}(p_{\langle r,s\rangle}-\varepsilon i\hat{b})\right)}{\gamma\left(\frac{3\hat{b}^{2}-1}{2}\right)\gamma\left(\varepsilon i\hat{b}(p_{\langle r,s\rangle}-\varepsilon i\hat{b}^{-1})\right)}\right]^{1/2}\ ,\quad\quad\varepsilon=\pm,

\displaystyle\left.C_{(1,3)(r,s)}\text{}^{(r,s+2\varepsilon)}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}\equiv\widetilde{C}_{(1,3)(r,s)}\text{}^{(r,s)}

(132)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~=\frac{i\hat{b}^{-2}\gamma\left(\frac{1+\hat{b}^{2}}{2}\right)\gamma\left(\frac{1+\hat{b}^{2}}{2}-i\hat{b}p_{\langle r,s\rangle}\right)}{\gamma\left(\frac{1-\hat{b}^{2}}{2}-i\hat{b}p_{\langle r,s\rangle}\right)}\left[\frac{\gamma\left(1-\hat{b}^{2}\right)\gamma\left(\frac{\hat{b}^{2}-1}{2}\right)}{\gamma\left(\hat{b}^{2}-1\right)\gamma\left(\frac{3\hat{b}^{2}-1}{2}\right)}\right]^{1/2},

(133)

and
p_{\langle r,s\rangle}
is defined as in (
94
). It is straightforward to check that these expressions agree with (
128
) under the normalization (
3.3
). Nevertheless, it is manifest that the timelike Liouville formulas do
not
return zero for operator dimensions different than the ones corresponding to the modules
\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s+2\varepsilon}\right]
with
\varepsilon=\pm,0
30
30

30

Here we have suppressed the even/odd notation for the modules in the OPE. It should be understood that
\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s\pm 2}\right]\equiv\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s\pm 2}\right]_{\mathrm{ee}}
whereas
\left.\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s+2\varepsilon}\right]\right|_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\equiv\left[\mathcal{O}_{r,s}\right]_{\mathrm{oo}}
, according to the notation in (
40
)-(
42
)
and hence there is no observed truncation of the fusion algebra in
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory, exactly as in the non-supersymmetric case.

4
Summary & Applications

We now summarize the structure constants of
\mathcal{N}=1
spacelike and timelike Liouville theory in the natural normalization that we have studied so far. We then proceed to a discussion of applications and possible future directions.

Spacelike structure constants

In the NS-sector,

\displaystyle\langle V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}(0)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\rho_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1})\right)^{-1}[\delta(p_{1}-p_{2})+\delta(p_{1}+p_{2})]~,

\displaystyle\langle V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}(0)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}(1)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})~,

\displaystyle\langle W^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}(0)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}(1)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})~,

(134)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p)

\displaystyle:=-4\sin(\pi bp)\sin(\pi b^{-1}p)\ ,

\displaystyle C_{\rm NS}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(2Q)}{2\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(Q)^{3}}\frac{\Gamma^{\rm NS}_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm p_{1}\pm p_{2}\pm p_{3}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}\left(Q\pm 2p_{j}\right)}~,

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}^{(b)}_{\rm NS}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})

\displaystyle:=i\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(2Q)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(Q)^{3}}\frac{\Gamma^{\rm R}_{b}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm p_{1}\pm p_{2}\pm p_{3}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}\left(Q\pm 2p_{j}\right)}~.

(135)

In the R-sector,

\displaystyle\langle V^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{p_{1}}(0)V^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{p_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\rho_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p_{1})\right)^{-1}[\delta(p_{1}-p_{2})\pm\delta(p_{1}+p_{2})]~,

\displaystyle\langle V^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{p_{1}}(0)V^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{p_{2}}(1)V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})\pm C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})~,

(136)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{R}}^{(b)}(p)

\displaystyle:=2\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi bp)\cos(\pi b^{-1}p)\ ,

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(2Q)}{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(Q)^{3}}\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm(p_{1}+p_{2})\pm p_{3}\right)\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm(p_{1}-p_{2})\pm p_{3}\right)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q\pm 2p_{1})\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q\pm 2p_{2})\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(Q\pm 2p_{3})},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(2Q)}{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{b}^{\rm NS}(Q)^{3}}\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm(p_{1}+p_{2})\pm p_{3}\right)\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}\left(\frac{Q}{2}\pm(p_{1}-p_{2})\pm p_{3}\right)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q\pm 2p_{1})\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q\pm 2p_{2})\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(Q\pm 2p_{3})}~.

(137)

Timelike structure constants

In the NS-sector,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(ip_{1})}{\hat{p}_{1}^{2}}[\delta(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})+\delta(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})]~,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{i}{\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})}~,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{i}{C^{(\hat{b})}_{\text{NS}}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2},i\hat{p}_{3})}~.

(138)

In the R-sector,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\rangle

\displaystyle=\rho_{\mathrm{R}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1})[\delta(\hat{p}_{1}-\hat{p}_{2})\pm\delta(\hat{p}_{1}+\hat{p}_{2})]~,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{1}}(0)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{R},\pm}_{\hat{p}_{2}}(1)\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}_{3}}(\infty)\rangle

\displaystyle=\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})\pm\,\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})~,

(139)

with

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})\equiv\pm\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})}~,

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2};\hat{p}_{3})\equiv\pm\frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(\hat{b})}(i\hat{p}_{1},i\hat{p}_{2};i\hat{p}_{3})}~.

(140)

We have explicitly kept the sign ambiguity in the expressions (
138
), (
140
) for the structure constants. We now highlight several promising directions to explore given our results.

\mathcal{N}=1
Virasoro minimal string.

There is at least one possible application of
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike and spacelike Liouville theory in constructing solvable models of string theory
31
31

31

We are especially grateful to Lorenz Eberhardt and Mukund Rangamani for discussions on this topic.
. In
Collier:2023cyw
the Virasoro minimal string (VMS) was introduced as a stringy realization of JT gravity. The Virasoro minimal string is a two-dimensional critical string theory, which from a worldsheet perspective corresponds to a spacelike and timelike non-supersymmetric Liouville CFT coupled to each other. The main observables in the VMS, the string amplitudes, were denoted as
\mathsf{V}_{g,n}^{(b)}
and are polynomials in the Liouville momenta and central charge. In the semiclassical limit (namely, at large spacelike Liouville central charge) the string amplitudes reduce to the standard Weil–Petersson volumes
Saad:2019lba
, hence in
Collier:2023cyw

\mathsf{V}_{g,n}^{(b)}
were interpreted as quantum volumes. The VMS furthermore exhibits a dual Hermitian double scaled matrix integral, whose topological recursion provides access to the quantum volumes for arbitrary genus
g
and number of punctures
n
.

It is tempting to imagine a
\mathcal{N}=1
extension of the VMS that consists of a worldsheet theory that couples in the same way the spacelike and timelike
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville CFTs, as described in the present work. Here we refrain from giving a complete presentation of the
\mathcal{N}=1
VMS but merely want to present an appetizer, and leave a more detailed discussion to future work. From a worldhseet perspective the
\mathcal{N}=1
VMS is the following superstring theory (see also
Johnson:2024fkm
)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{$c\geqslant 9$ $\mathcal{N}=1$}\\
\text{Liouville CFT}\end{array}\ \oplus\ \begin{array}[]{c}\text{$\hat{c}\leqslant 1$ $\mathcal{N}=1$}\\
\text{Liouville CFT}\end{array}\ \oplus\ \text{$\mathfrak{b}\mathfrak{c}$-ghosts}\ \oplus\ \text{$\beta\gamma$-ghosts}~.

(141)

In the above,
c\geqslant 9
is the quantum spacelike
\mathcal{N}=1
super Liouville CFT whereas
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
is the quantum timelike
\mathcal{N}=1
super Liouville CFT.
Imposing the vanishing of the conformal anomaly of the combined superstring theory (
141
)
we obtain the constraint

c+\hat{c}-10=0~\Leftrightarrow~\hat{b}=b~.

(142)

We now introduce the standard picture number convention, i.e.
-1
for the NS-sector and
-1/2
for the R-sector. Explicitly, if we denote by
\mathrm{e}^{q\varphi}
the “bosonized”
\beta,\gamma
-system with conformal dimension
h_{q}=-q(q+2)/2

deLacroix:2017lif
, we construct

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{N}(p)\mathfrak{c}\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi}\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{\varphi}}V^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{ip}~,

(143a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{R}}_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}(p)\mathfrak{c}\tilde{\mathfrak{c}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}}{2}}(V_{p}^{\mathrm{R},+}\widehat{V}_{ip}^{\mathrm{R},-}+iV_{p}^{\mathrm{R},-}\widehat{V}_{ip}^{\mathrm{R},+})~.

(143b)

where
\mathsf{N}(p),\mathsf{R}(p)
are some arbitrary normalizations. The above vertex operators satisfy the mass-shell conditions

h_{p}^{\mathrm{NS}}+\hat{h}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}}=\frac{1}{2}~,\quad h_{p}^{\mathrm{R}}+\hat{h}^{\mathrm{R}}_{\hat{p}}=\frac{5}{8}~,

(144)

which imply
\hat{p}=ip
.
Combining the mass-shell condition with (
142
) we obtain

4h_{p}^{\mathrm{R}}-\frac{c}{4}=-\left(4\hat{h}_{p}^{\mathrm{R}}-\frac{\hat{c}}{4}\right)~.

(145)

The choice of the Ramond state in (
143b
) guarantees that it is annihilated by the total holomorphic supercharge
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tot}}\equiv\mathcal{G}_{0}+\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0}
(and similarly for the total antiholomorphic one
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{tot}}\equiv\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{0}+\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{0}
)
32
32

32

To see this we use (
35
) and (
97
) combined with (
145
) and choose
\sqrt{-1}=i
.
. Alternatively, one could have chosen the Ramond state with a relative minus sign in (
143b
), in which case it would be annihilated by
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tot}}\equiv\mathcal{G}_{0}-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{0}
(and similarly for
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{tot}}
), corresponding to a different but consistent way of gauging the worldsheet theory. These two different choices, however, seem to lead to inequivalent string theories, even with a given GSO projection.

Indeed, the above defines a type 0B superstring theory. In type 0A there is in fact no R-sector vertex operator
Klebanov:2003wg
;
Seiberg:2003nm
.

We can now look at some first simple string amplitudes.
Using the notation
\mathsf{V}^{(b)}_{g,n_{\mathrm{NS}},n_{\mathrm{R}}}
, we consider the NS-NS-NS and R-R-NS sphere amplitudes

\mathsf{V}_{0,3,0}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})~\quad\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\quad\mathsf{V}_{0,1,2}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})~.

(146)

The dimension of the corresponding super-moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n_{\mathrm{NS}},n_{\mathrm{R}}}^{\mathcal{N}=1}
is given by (see e.g.
Witten:2012ga
)

\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n_{\mathrm{NS}},n_{\mathrm{R}}}^{\mathcal{N}=1})=3g-3+n_{\mathrm{NS}}+n_{\mathrm{R}}|2g-2+n_{\mathrm{NS}}+\frac{1}{2}n_{\mathrm{R}}~,

(147)

and therefore

\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{0,3,0}^{\mathcal{N}=1})=0|1~,\quad\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{0,1,2}^{\mathcal{N}=1})=0|0~.

(148)

To evaluate the string amplitudes (
146
) we will use the NSR formalism and replace the integration over the worldsheet gravitino by a picture changing operator (PCO)
Friedan:1985ge
. On a genus
g
surface the number of
\beta
minus the number of
\gamma
zero modes is
2g-2
, or equivalently the total
\varphi
charge must be
2g-2
. Therefore, on a sphere we have net picture number
(-2,-2)
. To achieve this, on a given amplitude with
n_{\mathrm{NS}}
number of
\mathrm{NS}
punctures and
n_{\mathrm{R}}
number of
\mathrm{R}
‘punctures’, we need to introduce
2g-2+n_{\mathrm{NS}}+\frac{1}{2}n_{\mathrm{R}}
number of (holomorphic) PCOs. Therefore, for the amplitudes (
146
) we need one (+ one antiholomorphic) PCO for
\mathsf{V}_{0,3,0}^{(b)}
and none for
\mathsf{V}_{0,1,2}^{(b)}
. Denoting the holomorphic PCO by
\chi

Friedan:1985ge
;
Balthazar:2022atu
we hence consider

\mathsf{V}_{0,3,0}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=\langle\chi\tilde{\chi}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}\rangle~,\quad\mathsf{V}_{0,1,2}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=\langle\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{1}}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{R}}_{p_{2}}\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}\rangle~.

(149)

Following standard methods (see e.g.
Balthazar:2022atu
;
Belavin:2008vc
) we find

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{0,3,0}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{N}(p_{1})\mathsf{N}(p_{2})\mathsf{N}(p_{3})\bigg[\langle V_{p_{1}}^{\mathrm{NS}}V_{p_{2}}^{\mathrm{NS}}V_{p_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\langle\widehat{W}_{ip_{1}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}_{ip_{2}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}_{ip_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle

(150)

\displaystyle\quad+\langle W_{p_{1}}^{\mathrm{NS}}V_{p_{2}}^{\mathrm{NS}}V_{p_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\langle\widehat{V}_{ip_{1}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}_{ip_{2}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\widehat{V}_{ip_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\bigg]

(151)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{N}(p_{1})\mathsf{N}(p_{2})\mathsf{N}(p_{3})\bigg[C_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(ip_{1},ip_{2},ip_{3})

(152)

\displaystyle\quad+\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\widehat{{C}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(b)}(ip_{1},ip_{2},ip_{3})\bigg]~.

(153)

Similarly we obtain for the R-R-NS three point amplitude

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{0,1,2}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}(p_{1})\mathsf{R}(p_{2})\mathsf{N}(p_{3})\bigg[\langle V_{p_{1}}^{\mathrm{R},+}V_{p_{2}}^{\mathrm{R},+}V_{p_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\langle\widehat{V}_{ip_{1}}^{\mathrm{R},-}\widehat{V}_{ip_{2}}^{\mathrm{R},-}\widehat{V}_{ip_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle

(154)

\displaystyle\quad-\langle V_{p_{1}}^{\mathrm{R},-}V_{p_{2}}^{\mathrm{R},-}V_{p_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\langle\widehat{V}_{ip_{1}}^{\mathrm{R},+}\widehat{V}_{ip_{2}}^{\mathrm{R},+}\widehat{V}_{ip_{3}}^{\mathrm{NS}}\rangle\bigg]

(155)

\displaystyle=2\mathsf{R}(p_{1})\mathsf{R}(p_{2})\mathsf{N}(p_{3})\bigg[C_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(ip_{1},ip_{2};ip_{3})

(156)

\displaystyle\quad-C_{\mathrm{even}}^{(b)}(p_{1},p_{2};p_{3})\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{odd}}^{(b)}(ip_{1},ip_{2};ip_{3})\bigg]~.

(157)

We are now facing an interesting puzzle. Notice that all the hatted quantities in (
153
), (
157
) –
corresponding to the
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike structure constants – are given exactly by the inverses of the spacelike structure constants that they multiply, as in (
138
), (
140
). However, as we explained in sections
2
and
3
, the structure constants of
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville theory can be determined from the basic shift relations only up to an overall sign ambiguity and hence, from the point of view of a
single
Liouville CFT, there is really no distinction for either sign. On the other hand, when we couple two
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville CFTs to define (
141
), the corresponding string amplitudes clearly depend on the relative signs, as these can determine whether the net result in (
153
), (
157
) is non-zero or vanishes entirely
33
33

33

It is useful to contrast this with the ordinary VMS case, where a similar sign ambiguity arises in the timelike DOZZ formula but ultimately plays no significant role, as there is only a single structure constant in that case.
.

Let us explore some expectations that might guide our interpretation. In the case of
\mathcal{N}=1
JT supergravity,
Stanford:2019vob
;
Turiaci:2024cad
argued that the presence of fermionic moduli forces the super Weil–Petersson volumes
V_{0,n_{\mathrm{NS}},0}=0
to vanish. If we interpret
\mathcal{N}=1
VMS as the superstringy realization of
\mathcal{N}=1
JT gravity one may be inclined to believe that also
\mathsf{V}^{(b)}_{0,n_{\mathrm{NS}},0}=0
34
34

34

Of course it is understood that this argument is a bit fast, since the ‘quantum super-volumes’ of
\mathcal{N}=1
VMS can in general be non-trivial and still yield something vanishing in the JT limit.
, and hence choose accordingly the relative signs in (
153
). On the other hand, in
Collier:2023cyw
the quantum volume corresponding to the sphere amplitude with three punctures was interpreted (through the connection with chiral three-dimensional gravity) as counting the number of Liouville sphere three-point blocks, which simply is unity. To back this even more, from a Random Matrix Theory perspective the fact that
\mathsf{V}_{0,3}^{(b)}=1
is a universal consequence of a ‘square root edge’ spectral curve
35
35

35

A ‘square root edge’ double-scaled random matrix has a universal form for the leading three-point correlator of resolvents, usually denoted by
\omega_{0,3}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
, which after Laplace transforming in
z_{1,2,3}
yields
\mathsf{V}_{0,3}(l_{1},l_{2},l_{3})=1
.
which is also true e.g. in JT gravity
Saad:2019lba
.
For the
\mathcal{N}=1
VMS case, in the NS-sector of Liouville theory there are two independent structure constants and hence one might believe that the net result of (
153
) should instead be non-trivial and equal to 2 (modulo obvious normalizations). This would consequently lead to yet a different choice of the relative signs in the above expression.
Similarly, for the case of the R-R-NS amplitude, and following the intuition of the corresponding vanishing amplitude in
\mathcal{N}=1
JT
Stanford:2019vob
, it could be possible that
\mathsf{V}^{(b)}_{0,1,2}=0
. However this does not seem to be compatible with the existence of a single Ramond sector Liouville conformal block according to the previous logic, thereby leading to an expected non-trivial net result for (
157
).

These arguments collectively seem to suggest that possibly either choice of relative signs in (
153
) and (
157
) may yield a viable string theory, reflecting eventually the two distinct ways of gauging the two
\mathcal{N}=1
algebras present on the worldsheet (exactly in the sense that we described below (
145
)). We leave a clearer understanding and resolutions of these interesting puzzles to future work
upcoming
.

Sphere partition function from timelike structure constants.

In
Anninos:2023exn
a systematic semiclassical expansion of the sphere path integral of
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory was established. In particular, the sphere partition function of timelike Liouville theory reads

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathcal{N}=1}_{\mathrm{tL}}[\mu]=\int\frac{[\mathcal{D}\phi][\mathcal{D}\psi]}{\mathrm{vol}_{OSP(1|2;\mathbb{C})}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-S_{\mathrm{tL}}^{\mathcal{N}=1}}~,

(158)

where the action is given by (
90
). The background metric
\tilde{\mathrm{e}}_{\mu}^{a}
, related to the physical metric by
\mathrm{e}_{\mu}^{a}=\mathrm{e}^{\hat{b}\phi}\tilde{\mathrm{e}}_{\mu}^{a}
, is now a round metric on the sphere with radius
r
leading to
\widetilde{R}=2/r^{2}
in (
90
). The path integral (
158
) admits a real two-sphere saddle and a systematic non-vanishing semiclassical loop expansion. We can ask how our explicit expressions for the
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike structure constants compare with existing approaches in the literature that evaluate the path integral. In particular, we can ask whether the following equality holds

\partial^{3}_{\mu}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathcal{N}=1}_{\mathrm{tL}}[\mu]\approx\widehat{\widetilde{C}}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}^{*},\hat{p}^{*},\hat{p}^{*})~,\quad\quad\text{for}~~\hat{p}^{*}=\frac{i}{2}(\hat{b}^{-1}+\hat{b})~,

(159)

where the approximation indicates equality up to terms independent of
\hat{b}
. The RHS of (
159
) encodes the three-point correlator
\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}\rangle
36
36

36

In the timelike theory we have
\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p}=\mu\mathrm{e}^{(\hat{\alpha}+\hat{b})\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\alpha}\overline{\psi}\psi\mathrm{e}^{\hat{\alpha}\phi}
where
\hat{\alpha}=-\frac{\widehat{Q}}{2}-i\hat{p}
.
which, via superconformal Ward identities
Belavin:2007gz
, is related to the correlator of
\langle\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{1}}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{2}}\widehat{V}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{p_{3}}\rangle
.
The choice
\hat{p}=\hat{p}^{*}
captures the operator

\widehat{W}^{\mathrm{NS}}_{\hat{p}^{*}}=\mu\mathrm{e}^{2\hat{b}\phi}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{b}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{b}\phi}\overline{\psi}\psi~,

(160)

which is the analog of the area operator in the non-supersymmetric case.
The analogous problem in the
\mathcal{N}=1
spacelike case was examined in
Anninos:2023exn
where it was shown that a non-vanishing prediction coming from the semiclassical expansion of the explicit expression for the structure constant (i.e. the analogous of the RHS of (
159
)) can be matched with the semiclassical path integral expansion coming from the LHS to one-loop order. In the timelike case however, it is straightforward to compute from our expressions (
138
) that
37
37

37

Note that, for the other structure constant,
\lim_{\hat{p}_{2}\rightarrow\hat{p}_{1}}\lim_{\hat{p}_{3}\rightarrow\hat{p}^{*}}\widehat{C}_{\mathrm{NS}}^{(\hat{b})}(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2},\hat{p}_{3})
diverges.

\widehat{\widetilde{C}}^{(\hat{b})}_{\rm NS}(\hat{p}^{*},\hat{p}^{*},\hat{p}^{*})=0~.

(161)

Just as in the non-supersymmetric case
Anninos:2021ene
, this result contradicts the non-vanishing semiclassical gravity calculation. It is still an interesting open problem to reconcile these two pictures in the timelike case, both for the non-supersymmetric and
\mathcal{N}
=1 setups.

\mathcal{N}=2
Liouville theories.

It is a natural step to include more supersymmetry. It has been shown that
\mathcal{N}=2
Liouville theory is related by mirror symmetry to the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})/U(1)
Kazama–Suzuki supercoset (the 2D fermionic “cigar” black hole)
Hori:2001ax
. Such a duality does not exist for the timelike (
c<3
) theory
Anninos:2023exn
. The general obstruction toward the structure constants of the
\mathcal{N}=2
Liouville theory partly has its roots on the fact that the classical background charge
Q=1/b
does not receive quantum corrections, and hence the quantum central charge does not end up having the
b\leftrightarrow b^{-1}
symmetry
38
38

38

In particular,
c^{\mathcal{N}=2}=3+6b^{-2}
.
that we are used to from the
\mathcal{N}=0,1
cases. It would be interesting to study what kind of alternative methods could give us access to the theory, such as supersymmetric localization
Anninos:2023exn
. In particular, it seems reasonable to expect that any potential analogue of the shift relations (which would originate purely from the symmetry algebra) would still be analytic as functions of
b
—even though there is no
b^{-1}
counterpart. As such, one can analytically continue these relations and subsequently study the resulting solutions, subject to the usual uniqueness arguments (which might turn out to be tricky in this case).

Crossing symmetry in
\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville.

A crucial step toward fully establishing

\mathcal{N}=1
timelike Liouville theory as a two-dimensional superconformal field theory is to demonstrate that the structure constants derived in this work give rise to crossing-symmetric four-point functions on the sphere, as well as modular-covariant one-point functions on the torus. As we discussed in Section
3
, the expected primary spectrum over which crossing/modular covariance will be established should be

\hat{p}\in i\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}-\epsilon

(162)

for both the NS and the Ramond sectors (c.f. (
92
)). This was checked numerically for some cases in
Mukund
, following similar analysis as in the non-supersymmetric case
Ribault:2015sxa
. What is still missing, both in the bosonic and
\mathcal{N}=1
case, is an analytic derivation of these statements. A key component in this direction is the construction of the fusion and modular kernels for
\hat{c}\leqslant 1
(as continuous functions of
\hat{b}
), which have yet to be developed for either case
39
39

39

See however
Ribault:2023vqs
,
Roussillon:2024wmr
for the non-supersymmetric case.
.
In particular we emphasize that, at least in the spacelike case, the Liouville structure constants (in the natural normalization) are particular instances of these kernels (see Appendix
E
)
Teschner:2001rv
;
Poghosyan:2016kvd
. The latter, in turn, are solutions to Moore-Seiberg consistency conditions which can be recast as shift relations that uniquely determine the kernels in the corresponding spacelike and timelike regimes of the central charge
Eberhardt:2023mrq
;
Ribault:2023vqs
. It would be worthwhile to explore further the analogous statements for the timelike case in the future.

Appendix A
Special functions

In this appendix we review the special functions necessary for the construction of the structure constants in
\mathcal{N}=0,1
Liouville theory.

\mathcal{N}=0

We start by briefly mentioning the special functions relevant for the structure constants of ordinary bosonic Liouville theory. More specific details and further properties of these functions are well-documented and can be found, e.g., in
Harlow:2011ny
;
Collier:2018exn
.

The main special function is the Barnes double gamma function
\Gamma_{b}(x)
with the integral representation

\log\Gamma_{b}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\text{d}t\over t}\left[{\mathrm{e}^{-xt}-\mathrm{e}^{-Qt/2}\over(1-\mathrm{e}^{-bt})(1-\mathrm{e}^{-b^{-1}t})}-{1\over 2}(Q/2-x)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t}-{Q/2-x\over t}\right]

(163)

convergent for
x
in the right half-plane. Away from this region it is defined via analytic continuation from its shift relations in
b,b^{-1}

\frac{\Gamma_{b}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Gamma_{b}(x)}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}b^{\pm b^{\pm 1}x\mp\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(b^{\pm 1}x)},

(164)

where we implicitly assume that
b^{2}\notin\mathbb{Q}
40
40

40

In this case the two shift relations in
b
and
b^{-1}
are incommensurable.
. As a function of
b
, it is analytic in the whole
b^{2}-
complex
plane
except for the negative part of the real axis, where it meets with a natural bound of
analyticity. It also has the property
\Gamma_{b}=\Gamma_{b^{-1}}
. As a function of
x
, it is meromorphic with no zeroes, and simple poles at
x=-mb-nb^{-1}
, for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
41
41

41

\mathbb{N}
here (and for the rest of this appendix) stands for the natural numbers
including
zero.
.

There are two main special functions built out of
\Gamma_{b}(x)
:

S_{b}(x):=\frac{\Gamma_{b}(x)}{\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)},\quad\Upsilon_{b}(x):=\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}(x)\Gamma_{b}(Q-x)}~.

(165)

From the properties of
\Gamma_{b}
it is straightforward to deduce all the analytic properties of these functions. For example their corresponding shift relations read

\frac{S_{b}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{S_{b}(x)}=2\sin{(\pi b^{\pm 1}x)}\ ,~~\frac{\Upsilon_{b}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Upsilon_{b}(x)}=b^{\pm 1\mp 2b^{\pm 1}x}\frac{\Gamma(b^{\pm 1}x)}{\Gamma(1-b^{\pm 1}x)}~.

(166)

\mathcal{N}=1

The relevant special functions for
\mathcal{N}=1
Liouville theory are again constructed from
\Gamma_{b}
, but are characterized by a slightly different analytic structure. In particular, we again seek functions with simple poles on the grid
x=-mb-nb^{-1}
, except now the non-negative integer pairs
(m,n)
should be restricted to combinations of (even, even), (odd, odd), or (even, odd). This particular structure is captured by a product of two
\Gamma_{b}
’s. The two corresponding functions
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}
and
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}
are defined as

\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)\equiv\Gamma_{b}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)\Gamma_{b}\left(\frac{x+b+b^{-1}}{2}\right)\ ,\ \ \ \Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)\equiv\Gamma_{b}\left(\frac{x+b}{2}\right)\Gamma_{b}\left(\frac{x+b^{-1}}{2}\right)~.

(167)

It is obvious that
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}(\mathrm{R})}=\Gamma_{b^{-1}}^{\mathrm{NS}(\mathrm{R})}
.
As a function of
x
,
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}
and
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}
have simple poles at

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ x=-kb-lb^{-1}~,~~~~\ (k,l)\in(2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0})\text{ or }(2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}+1,2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}+1)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ x=-k^{\prime}b-l^{\prime}b^{-1}~,~\ (k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in(2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}+1)\text{ or }(2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}+1,2\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}).

(168)

Their
shift relations
are deduced from (
164
) and read

\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\ b^{\pm\frac{xb^{\pm 1}}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+b^{\pm 1}x}{2}\right)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\ b^{\mp\frac{1}{2}(1-b^{\pm 1}x)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{xb^{\pm 1}}{2}\right)}.

(169)

Notice that shifts in
b^{\pm 1}
necessarily “couple” the two different functions
\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}},\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}
with each other. We would need to have shifts by either
2b
,
2b^{-1}
,
Q=b+b^{-1}
, or
\hat{Q}=b^{-1}-b
to get shift relations between the same functions. For example, for shifts by
Q
we get

\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x+Q)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=\frac{2\pi\ b^{\frac{1}{2}\left(bx-b^{-1}x+2\right)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{xb^{-1}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{bx}{2}\right)},\quad\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x+Q)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}=\frac{2\pi\ b^{\frac{1}{2}(bx-b^{-1}x)}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1+b^{-1}x}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1+bx}{2}\right)}.

(170)

S^{\mathrm{NS}}
and
S^{\mathrm{R}}
.

In analogy with the
\mathcal{N}=0
case, we further introduce two analogs of the
S_{b}
function as follows:

S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x):=\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(Q-x)}\ ,\ \ \ S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x):=\frac{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}{\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q-x)}~.

(171)

They have
poles
at

\displaystyle S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=-kb-lb^{-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ k,l\in\mathbb{N}\ |\ (k,l)\in(2\mathbb{N},2\mathbb{N})\text{ or }(2\mathbb{N}+1,2\mathbb{N}+1)

\displaystyle S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)^{-1}

\displaystyle=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=-k^{\prime}b-l^{\prime}b^{-1}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\ |\ (k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in(2\mathbb{N},2\mathbb{N}+1)\text{ or }(2\mathbb{N}+1,2\mathbb{N}).

(172)

They have
zeroes
at

\displaystyle S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)

\displaystyle=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=kb+lb^{-1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ \ k,l\in\mathbb{N}\ |\ (k,l)\in(2\mathbb{N}+1,2\mathbb{N}+1)\text{ or }(2\mathbb{N}+2,2\mathbb{N}+2)

\displaystyle S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)

\displaystyle=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=k^{\prime}b+l^{\prime}b^{-1}\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\ |\ (k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in(2\mathbb{N}+2,2\mathbb{N}+1)\text{ or }(2\mathbb{N}+1,2\mathbb{N}+2).

(173)

They satisfy the
shift relations

\frac{S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}=2\cos{\left(\frac{\pi b^{\pm 1}x}{2}\right)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \frac{S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=2\sin{\left(\frac{\pi b^{\pm 1}x}{2}\right)}.

(174)

Shifts by
Q
yield

\frac{S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x+Q)}{S_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=-4\sin\left(\frac{\pi bx}{2}\right)\sin\left(\frac{\pi b^{-1}x}{2}\right)\ \ ,\ \ \ \ \ \frac{S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x+Q)}{S_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}=4\cos\left(\frac{\pi bx}{2}\right)\cos\left(\frac{\pi b^{-1}x}{2}\right).

(175)

\Upsilon^{\mathrm{NS}}
and
\Upsilon^{\mathrm{R}}
.

Finally we introduce two analogs of the
\Upsilon_{b}
function as follows:

\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma^{\mathrm{NS}}_{b}(x)\Gamma^{\mathrm{NS}}_{b}(Q-x)}~,\ \ \ \Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}^{R}(x)\Gamma_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(Q-x)}~.

(176)

These are entire functions with
zeroes
at

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=kb+lb^{-1},\ \ \text{for}\ \ k,l\in\mathbb{Z}\ |\ \text{sgn}(k\cdot l)=1|\ (k,l)\in(2\mathbb{Z},2\mathbb{Z})\text{ or }(2\mathbb{Z}+1,2\mathbb{Z}+1),

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ x=k^{\prime}b+l^{\prime}b^{-1},\ \text{for}\ k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\ |\ \text{sgn}(k^{\prime}\cdot l^{\prime})=1|\ (k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in(2\mathbb{Z},2\mathbb{Z}+1)\text{ or }(2\mathbb{Z},2\mathbb{Z}+1).

(177)

They satisfy the
shift relations

\frac{\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x)}=b^{\mp b^{\pm 1}x}\gamma\left(\frac{1+xb^{\pm 1}}{2}\right)\ ,\ \ \ \ \frac{\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{R}}(x+b^{\pm 1})}{\Upsilon_{b}^{\mathrm{NS}}(x)}=b^{\pm(1-b^{\pm 1}x)}\gamma\left(\frac{xb^{\pm 1}}{2}\right),

(178)

where
\gamma(x)\equiv\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(1-x)}
.

Appendix B
Review of the analytic bootstrap in
\mathcal{N}=0
Liouville CFT

In this appendix we give a pedagogical and self-contained review of the derivation of the structure constants for ordinary bosonic Liouville CFT with central charge
c\geqslant 25
(spacelike) and
c\leqslant 1
(timelike). In particular, we follow the chain of reasoning that we presented in the main body of the paper for the
\mathcal{N}=1
case, implementing Teschner’s trick and emphasizing the importance of shift relations for the normalization-independent bootstrap data as well as their symmetries under Virasoro-Wick Rotation.

For the purposes of this appendix we use the following notations for the central charge and conformal dimensions:

c=1+6Q^{2},~~~~~Q=b+b^{-1},~~~~~h=\frac{Q^{2}}{4}-p^{2}\ .

(179)

The physical spectrum of bosonic Liouville consists of scalar primary operators with
p\in i\mathbb{R}
.
Degenerate representations of the Virasoro algebra occur at (real) momenta
\pm p_{\langle r,s\rangle}
, where

p_{\langle r,s\rangle}=\frac{1}{2}\left(rb+sb^{-1}\right)\ ,\ \ r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}\ .

(180)

Derivation of the shift-relations

We consider the analytically continued (i.e. outside Liouville theory’s spectrum) four-point function on the sphere

\langle V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}(z_{0})V_{p_{1}}(z_{1})V_{p_{2}}(z_{2})V_{p_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle\ ,

(181)

with a single insertion of a degenerate operator
V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}
and three physical operators with
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in i\mathbb{R}
. The degenerate operator gives rise to a null vector at level 2

\left[\frac{1}{b^{2}}L_{-1}^{2}+L_{-2}\right]V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}=0.

(182)

The usual way that this null state equation leads to the BPZ equation
Belavin:1984vu
is as follows.

Since
T(z)=O(z^{-4}
) as
z\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\oint_{\infty}\text{d}z\ \varepsilon(z)T(z)=0
for any holomorphic function
\varepsilon(z)
with the property
\varepsilon(z)=O(z^{n})
, and
n\leqslant 2
, as
z\rightarrow\infty
. In the case of the four-point function (
181
), and since the OPE of primary fields with
T
behaves as

T(z)V_{p}(z_{i})=O((z-z_{i})^{-2}),

(183)

it is convenient to choose
\varepsilon(z)
to be a monomial in
(z-z_{i})
for
i=1,\cdots,3
and, to ensure the behaviour at infinity, we divide with the monomial in the remaining point. In other words, we choose
\varepsilon(z)=\frac{(z-z_{1})(z-z_{2})(z-z_{3})}{z-z_{0}}
. We then get the following Ward identity

\oint_{\infty}\text{d}z\ \varepsilon(z)\langle T(z)V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}(z_{0})V_{p_{1}}(z_{1})V_{p_{2}}(z_{2})V_{p_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle=0.

(184)

We next deform the contour to wrap all the circles around the points
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
and use the corresponding OPE. In other words,

\displaystyle\oint_{z_{0}}\text{d}z\ \varepsilon(z)\langle\left[T(z)V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}(z_{0})\right]_{\text{OPE}}V_{p_{1}}(z_{1})V_{p_{2}}(z_{2})V_{p_{3}}(z_{3})\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\oint_{z_{i}}\text{d}z\ \varepsilon(z)\langle V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}(z_{0})\bigg[T(z)V_{p_{i}}(z_{i})\bigg]_{\text{OPE}}\prod_{j\neq i}V_{p_{j}}(z_{j})\rangle=0\ .

(185)

p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}
p_{1}
p_{3}
p_{2}
=
p_{1}
p_{3}
p_{2}
p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}

Figure 7
:
Teschner’s trick in usual bosonic Liouville theory: the analytic bootstrap problem involving crossing of the sphere four-point function between a Virasoro degenerate field
V_{p_{\langle 2,1\rangle}}
and three basic fields
